[^fn-p0323-1]: (1) He was *praef. praet.* 141–157, and therefore, we may suppose, a personal friend of Pius.
[^fn-p0327-1]: (1) Fronto had pupils who lived with him, such as the two sons of Sardius Saturninus, mentioned below.
[^fn-p0327-2]: (2) This was the historian Appian, who tells us in the Preface to his *History* that he received such an appointment from Marcus and Lucius, when emperors. These procurators were set over every department of state and of the imperial household. They managed the emperor's domains, his mines, etc., the corn-supply, the water-supply, and the alimentary institutions. In the imperial provinces the procurators were fiscal officers. The procurator *a rationibus* was the highest of these officials, and corresponded to a Secretary of State.
[^fn-p0329-1]: (1) *i.e.* that the Greek as well as Appian should be granted his request.
[^fn-p0329-2]: (2) See Fronto’s letter throwing up his proconsulship, p. 236.
[^fn-p0329-3]: (3) It appears that Appian had sent Fronto a present of two slaves, which Fronto returned. Appian now sends them again, with this letter.
[^fn-p0331-1]: (1) Martial heads his thirteenth book of epigrams *Xenia*, from the little complimentary gifts made to guests and friends.
[^fn-p0341-1]: (1) At Athens a man, who thought himself unfairly taxed compared with another, could claim a re-assessment for both or an exchange of properties between them (ἀντιδόσις).
[^fn-p0343-1]: (1) Hom. *Il.* vi. 236.
[^fn-p0343-2]: (2) *cp.* Hesiod, *W. and D.* 349, 354: εὖ μὲν μετρεῖσθαι παρὰ γείτονος, εὖ δ' ἀποδοῦναι | αὐτῷ τῷ μέτρῳ.
[^fn-p0343-3]: (3) Proconsul of Africa 156-159. Apuleius also (*Apol.* 94 f.) wrote to Avitus a letter of recommendation, eulogizing him in language that reminds us of Fronto.
[^fn-p0345-1]: (1) Fronto tells us elsewhere (*Ad Ver.* ii. 7, and *cp.* Marcus, *Thoughts*, i. 11) that φιλοστοργία was practically non-existent, at least among the patricians of Rome. The word means affection between the members of a family.
[^fn-p0347-1]: (1) Corn. Repentinus Contuccius was *praef. praet.* with Fur. Victorinus for the year 159, and probably died that year. As the *praef. praet.* had judicial powers, the case of Fabianus may have come before him.
[^fn-p0347-2]: (2) Probably the consul of 146, and the father-in-law of Marcus's daughter Fadilla. In his *Thoughts*, i. 14, Marcus mentions the latter as “having confidence in the love of his friends.”
[^fn-p0349-1]: (1) Phrynichus in his Ἐκλογή speaks highly of a Sulp. Cornelianus, and says that Marcus and Lucius put all the affairs of the Greeks in his charge *συνεργὸν αὐτὸν ἑλόμενοι τῆς βασιλείας.*
[^fn-p0351-1]: (1) If the MS. *concilii* may be so translated. The *Consilium* was a body of officials and assessors attending the judges at a trial.
[^fn-p0353-1]: (1) Nothing is known for certain of him. Plarianus was *leg. pr. pr.* of Africa in 159. For him see *C.I.L.* viii. 800, 1177.
[^fn-p0353-2]: (2) *cp.* the use of *adparatus* in Hor. *Od.* i. 38. Dio, lxxii. 11, § 2, uses the expression παρασκευὴ τῶν λόγων.
[^fn-p0355-1]: (1) The other name of Julianus. He was consul in 145, and therefore proconsul about 157–159.
[^fn-p0357-1]: (1) Fronto was born at Cirta, now Constantine, in Numidia. Triumvirs, also in some cases *quattuorviri iuri dicundo*, were the chief magistrates of *municipia*. Colonies, such as Cirta, usually had *duumviri*.
[^fn-p0357-2]: (2) Gratia.
[^fn-p0359-1]: (1) Now Bona or Beled el Aneb.
[^fn-p0359-2]: (2) A grammarian of whom an inscr. (*C.I.L.* vi. 146) says *orator utraque facundia maximus.* For him see Aul. Gell. xix. 13.
[^fn-p0359-3]: (3) The official title of senators.
[^fn-p0361-1]: (1) This letter appears to have been written very soon after the death of Pius (on March 7, 161). Fronto had been away four months, possibly on a visit to Africa, where he had property and friends.
[^fn-p0363-1]: (1) Terence, *Phorm.* <span class="small-caps">iii</span>. i. 6. *Adelphi*, <span class="small-caps">iii</span>. ii. 32.
[^fn-p0365-1]: (1) The great-aunt of Marcus. One of the little daughters must have been Cornificia, born about 159. It is not clear who the other was. Domitia Faustina died before Marcus became emperor, and Sabina was not born yet.
[^fn-p0365-2]: (2) Lucilla and Fadilla.
[^fn-p0367-1]: (1) In 143. *cp.* above, p. 113.
[^fn-p0367-2]: (2) *cp.* Marcus, *Thoughts,* i. 16; vi. 30.
[^fn-p0369-1]: (1) Also called Pylades. They were both *pantomimi*.
[^fn-p0369-2]: (2) Probably a freedman of Verus, named after the great actor Apolaustus (mentioned *Vit. Veri*, viii.).
[^fn-p0371-1]: (1) Not Suetonius the writer, who would have been seventy years old by 139 <span class="small-caps">a.d.</span>
